## ADH*, G3PDH*, MDH-1*, MDH-2*, ME*, IDHP-1*, IDHP-2*, AAT-1*, CK-1*, EST-1*, GPI-

10 [SOURCE: Page_200]

B$_1$*, GPI-B$_2$*, PROT-1*, PROT-2* and PROT-3* were monomorphic. Only PGM-1*, MPI-2* [SOURCE: Page_201]

and LDH-C$_1$* revealed a polymorphism. [SOURCE: Page_201]

Allelic frequencies were computed for each sample but results for Rhône and Vaccarès samples [SOURCE: Page_201]

(n=4 and n=5 respectively) have to be interpreted with care because of the low number of [SOURCE: Page_201]

individuals (Table 5). LDH exhibited two strips which could be interpreted as two loci. [SOURCE: Page_201]

Nevertheless, some individuals displayed a strong spot on one of the strips whereas the other spot [SOURCE: Page_201]

was light or even absent. Consequently, a pattern interpretation was used noting A: one spot [SOURCE: Page_201]

above; B: a spot above and lighter one below; C: two equal spots; D: one spot below and one [SOURCE: Page_201]

lighter above; E: one spot below (Fig 4). Only one case of pattern A and one case of E were [SOURCE: Page_201]

recorded. Therefore, they were attributed to their nearest pattern for the statistical analysis (A [SOURCE: Page_201]

became B and E became D). Consequently, the genetic structure was interpretable for Bouic and [SOURCE: Page_201]

Fumemorte samples with PGM-1*, MPI-2* and LDH-C$_1$* and for the Rhone population for [SOURCE: Page_201]

LDH-C$_1$* only. [SOURCE: Page_201]

Observed heterozygosity using the same loci for the four sites, ranged between 0.013±0.05 [SOURCE: Page_201]

(Vaccarès) and 0.042±0.12 (Fumemorte). The range of the unbiased heterozygosity was more [SOURCE: Page_201]

reduced: from 0.035±0.14 (Rhône) to 0.052±0.15 (Fumemorte). For the Fumemorte and the [SOURCE: Page_201]

Vaccarès, observed heterozygosity was lower than expected, tending to indicate a departure from [SOURCE: Page_201]

Hardy-Weinberg expectations. This was confirmed by significant Fis which indicated a departure [SOURCE: Page_201]

from panmixia (Fumemorte: Fis=0.189, permutation test, 1000 permutations, p<0.05 and [SOURCE: Page_201]

Vaccarès: Fis=0.670, permutation test, 1000 permutations, p<0.001). The same was observed [SOURCE: Page_201]

when Fis was calculated for the four samples pooled (Fis=0.15, permutation test, 1000 [SOURCE: Page_201]

permutations, p<0.05). Consequently, the number of migrants (Nm) between the Fumemorte and [SOURCE: Page_201]

the Bouic Canal cannot be estimated using Wright's formula. [SOURCE: Page_201]

11 [SOURCE: Page_201]

Calculating the Fst, the only significant differences were observed between the Bouic and the [SOURCE: Page_202]

Rhône (Fst=0.20, permutation test, 1000 permutations, p<0.05) and the Fumemorte and the Bouic [SOURCE: Page_202]

(Fst=0.04, permutation test, 1000 permutations, p<0.05). Concerning the LDH-C$_1$* phenotype, in [SOURCE: Page_202]

order to assess whether the difference in distribution was due to ontogeny, we tested the pattern [SOURCE: Page_202]

distribution between populations with the length of fish (log-transformed) as a covariable. For [SOURCE: Page_202]

this purpose, we used a multinomial regression which showed that both the length (Chi²=13.3, [SOURCE: Page_202]

df=2, p<0.01) and the population (Chi²=21.3, df=6, p<0.01) explained significantly the LDH-C$_1$* [SOURCE: Page_202]

pattern. Thus, even though ontogeny influenced the LDH-C$_1$* pattern, the populations displayed [SOURCE: Page_202]

significant differences. On that basis, using Chi² test, we tested the differences between samples [SOURCE: Page_202]

(Table 6): Fumemorte and Bouic samples displayed significant differences in LDH-C$_1$* [SOURCE: Page_202]

frequencies likewise the Fumemorte and the Vaccarès. The Rhône sample did not show any [SOURCE: Page_202]

significant difference with the Bouic sample or with the Fumemorte sample. [SOURCE: Page_202]

Nevertheless, concerning the results about Rhône and the Vaccarès populations, the samples were [SOURCE: Page_202]

too small to make reliable interpretations (Table 5). [SOURCE: Page_202]

Phenotypic variations [SOURCE: Page_202]

Meristics [SOURCE: Page_202]

The stepwise procedure did not retain any variables. The permutation test performed with all the [SOURCE: Page_202]

variables showed no significant differences between sites, sexes or ages. [SOURCE: Page_202]

Landmark method [SOURCE: Page_202]

The stepwise analysis retained 6 PC from the centred PCA (PC$_4$; PC$_7$; PC12; PC19; PC28; [SOURCE: Page_202]

PC31) that significantly discriminated the Bouic sample from the Fumemorte sample [SOURCE: Page_202]

(permutations test, p<0.001). Using these variables, 77.2% fish were classified into their correct [SOURCE: Page_202]

12 [SOURCE: Page_202]

sample (cross-validation method). Misclassification was nearly the same for both sites (21.2% for [SOURCE: Page_203]

the Fumemorte sample and 24.4% for the Bouic sample) (Table 7). These variables did not allow [SOURCE: Page_203]

discrimination between sex (permutation test, p=0.12) or age classes (permutation test, p=0.59). [SOURCE: Page_203]

Using this model, all 5 supplementary individuals from the Vaccarès Lagoon were assigned to [SOURCE: Page_203]

the Fumemorte population. [SOURCE: Page_203]

Otolith shape [SOURCE: Page_203]

The stepwise analysis retained seven variables (Circularity; A$_5$; A12; B12; Φ7; Φ9; Φ13) that [SOURCE: Page_203]

significantly discriminated the Bouic from the Fumemorte samples (permutation test, p=0.018). [SOURCE: Page_203]

Discriminant analysis allowed 84.2% of the individuals to be correctly classified. [SOURCE: Page_203]

Misclassification was higher for the Fumemorte sample (20.0%) than for the Bouic sample [SOURCE: Page_203]

(9.4%) (Table 7). No difference in the otolith shape was noted between sexes using the seven [SOURCE: Page_203]

variables (permutation test, p=0.87) nor between age classes (permutation test, p=0.11). Among [SOURCE: Page_203]

the seven supplementary individuals from the Vaccarès Lagoon, 71% (5) were assigned to the [SOURCE: Page_203]

Fumemorte population. [SOURCE: Page_203]

Discussion [SOURCE: Page_203]

Samples size [SOURCE: Page_203]

The formerly abundant pikeperch population in the Fumemorte Canal is nowadays reduced: in [SOURCE: Page_203]

2001, only 28 adult pikeperch have been captured during four days of sampling per month with [SOURCE: Page_203]

fyke nets and two days with gill nets. Using the same protocol (except gill nets), 12 adults were [SOURCE: Page_203]

caught in the Bouic Canal in 2001. In the Vaccarès Lagoon, no adults were captured in 2001, [SOURCE: Page_203]

probably due to too high salinity (10 to 14 g/L). [SOURCE: Page_203]

13 [SOURCE: Page_203]

Thus the capture of a large number of individuals was quite difficult, first because it would have [SOURCE: Page_204]

required a very great sampling effort and second because it would threaten the pikeperch [SOURCE: Page_204]

populations. [SOURCE: Page_204]

Genetic variation in the populations [SOURCE: Page_204]

Few genetic studies of pikeperch have been reported and most of them concerned small samples [SOURCE: Page_204]

for phylogenetic purposes (BILLINGTON et al. 1990, FABER & STEPIEN 1997, BILLINGTON 1998, [SOURCE: Page_204]

FABER & STEPIEN 1998, NESBO et al. 1998). Thus, the study analysing the genetic structure of a [SOURCE: Page_204]

pikeperch population in the Baltic Sea and in a lagoon (PAULAUSKA & LOZYS 2001) is the only [SOURCE: Page_204]

one that could be taken as an informative reference. [SOURCE: Page_204]

Allozyme polymorphism in our samples was low, unlike the finding of PAULAUSKA & LOZYS [SOURCE: Page_204]

(2001) (4 polymorphic loci out of 7). BILLINGTON et al. (1990) also observed a low [SOURCE: Page_204]

polymorphism for pikeperch but their sample was small (2 individuals). As we have screened a [SOURCE: Page_204]

fair number of loci (i.e. 17 over 24 tested), the low number of polymorphic loci can be due to a [SOURCE: Page_204]

characteristic of the species when allozymes are used, as observed for the other percid species [SOURCE: Page_204]

like the saugere (Sander canadense) (BILLINGTON et al. 1990), the yellow perch (Perca [SOURCE: Page_204]

flavescens) (TODD & HATCHER 1993) and the European perch (Perca fluviatilis) (GYLLENSTEN et [SOURCE: Page_204]

al. 1985, HELDSTAB & KATOH 1995). It could be also due to a founding effect: the low diversity [SOURCE: Page_204]

and/or the small number of fish from which the Camargue population issued. [SOURCE: Page_204]

In our study the mean heterozygosity for the pooled samples was Hnb=0.043 which is in the same [SOURCE: Page_204]

range as that of other freshwater fish (He=0.046, (WARD et al. 1994). But it remains much lower [SOURCE: Page_204]

than the heterozygosity found by PAULAUSKA & LOZYS (2001) in the Curonian Lagoon and in the [SOURCE: Page_204]

Baltic Sea (Hnb=0.342 and 0.136, respectively, recalculated from the allelic frequencies). [SOURCE: Page_204]

However, the polymorphic loci found by PAULAUSKA & LOZYS (2001) were esterases which are [SOURCE: Page_204]

14 [SOURCE: Page_204]

quite difficult to interpret (BERREBI et al. 1990). So any comparison with our results should be [SOURCE: Page_205]

done with caution since the number of loci tested was lower and the electrophoresis method was [SOURCE: Page_205]

not the same (polyacrylamide gel). We cannot conclude as to the genetic variability of the [SOURCE: Page_205]

pikeperch population in the Camargue since there have been too few population genetic studies [SOURCE: Page_205]

of this species. [SOURCE: Page_205]

The genetic distance between the Bouic and the Fumemorte samples is small but significant, [SOURCE: Page_205]

suggesting a recent segregation of the populations. This is congruent with the results of the LDH- [SOURCE: Page_205]

C$_1$* pattern comparison that emphasises a significant difference between the irrigation and the [SOURCE: Page_205]

drainage pikeperch population. The LDH-C$_1$* pattern shows that the Rhône sample is [SOURCE: Page_205]

significantly different neither from the Bouic nor from the Fumemorte sample. But, in the case of [SOURCE: Page_205]

the Fumemorte sample, the p value was near the significance threshold (p=0.07) whereas in the [SOURCE: Page_205]

Bouic sample it was quite far (p=0.41). So we can presume that the Rhône population is [SOURCE: Page_205]

genetically closer to the irrigation population than to the drainage one. [SOURCE: Page_205]

The Fumemorte population shows a significant deficiency of heterozygotes which would suggest [SOURCE: Page_205]

a high inbreeding level, recent immigration of a particular selection phenomenon. This is not the [SOURCE: Page_205]

case for the Bouic sample and considering the LDH-C$_1$* pattern, it suggests that this population [SOURCE: Page_205]

has a continuous input from the Rhône population, which prevents inbreeding. [SOURCE: Page_205]

Phenotypic variation of the populations [SOURCE: Page_205]

The body morphology and the otolith shape significantly discriminated between the Bouic and [SOURCE: Page_205]

Fumemorte samples, which is congruent with the genetic results. This difference is not due to age [SOURCE: Page_205]

classes neither to a sexual dimorphism, which is consistent with a previous study (GOUBIER [SOURCE: Page_205]

1975). In addition, meristic characters are less efficient discriminators than morphometric ones [SOURCE: Page_205]

(MENG & STOCKER 1984, WALDMAN et al. 1997, HURLBUT & CLAY 1998). [SOURCE: Page_205]

15 [SOURCE: Page_205]

In his biometric study of different French pikeperch populations (including the Vaccarès [SOURCE: Page_206]

Lagoon), (GOUBIER 1975) concluded as to the steadiness of the pikeperch morphometric features. [SOURCE: Page_206]

On the other hand, (KRPO CETKOVIC & STAMENKOVIC 1996) discriminated among four pikeperch [SOURCE: Page_206]

populations in the Danube using morphometric features. The absence of significant results in [SOURCE: Page_206]

(GOUBIER 1975) may be due to the statistical method employed: he compared the correlation [SOURCE: Page_206]

coefficient of the linear relationship between each non log-transformed feature and the total [SOURCE: Page_206]

length (or the head length) without performing any statistical tests between populations. [SOURCE: Page_206]

Using otolith shape or morphometric features, most of the pikeperch from the Vaccarès Lagoon [SOURCE: Page_206]

were assigned to the Fumemorte population. This supports the hypothesis that pikeperch from the [SOURCE: Page_206]

Vaccarès Lagoon come from the Fumemorte Canal and that there is a real phenotypic difference [SOURCE: Page_206]

between the irrigation compartment (Bouic) and the drainage compartment (Fumemorte and [SOURCE: Page_206]

Vaccarès). However, the Vaccarès sample is too small to definitely prove this. Unfortunately, we [SOURCE: Page_206]

failed to catch a sufficient number of adults from the Rhône River to have a phenotypic proof for [SOURCE: Page_206]

the Rhône origin of the Bouic population as the biochemical marker LDH-C$_1$* tends to indicate. [SOURCE: Page_206]

Causes of the genetic and phenotypic variations [SOURCE: Page_206]

The genetic and phenotypic results tend to show an isolation of the pikeperch population [SOURCE: Page_206]

inhabiting the Fumemorte Canal. [SOURCE: Page_206]

This could be explained by the recent history of the pikeperch population. This species appeared [SOURCE: Page_206]

in France in 1912 in the canal between the Rhône and the Rhine (GOUBIER 1972). However, it [SOURCE: Page_206]

remains unclear whether pikeperch came naturally from lake Constance or were introduced by [SOURCE: Page_206]

man, both hypotheses being probably true (GOUBIER 1972). In the 1930s the pikeperch went [SOURCE: Page_206]

down the Rhône River and then, in 1948-1949 it naturally colonised the brackish water (< 8 g/L) [SOURCE: Page_206]

of the Vaccarès Lagoon in the Camargue where it found suitable conditions to reproduce. [SOURCE: Page_206]

16 [SOURCE: Page_206]

Furthermore, after a sharp decrease of the numbers in 1960, the lagoon was restocked with fry in [SOURCE: Page_207]

1962, 1963 and 1964 but without success (KIENER 1968). In the autumn of 1981 after a severe [SOURCE: Page_207]

drought, the salinity rose to 15 g/L in the Vaccarès Lagoon, forcing the pikeperch population to [SOURCE: Page_207]

take refuge in the Fumemorte Canal. The salinity reached 35 g/L in 1984. At the beginning of the [SOURCE: Page_207]

1990s the population in the canal had almost collapsed due to the pressure of the numerous [SOURCE: Page_207]

anglers and to lack of recruitment (unpublished data). During the winters of 1990 and 1991, a [SOURCE: Page_207]

total of 1049 young of the year from a hatchery, were marked (oxytetracycline and clip) and [SOURCE: Page_207]

stocked in the Fumemorte Canal (GAAMOUR 1993). Only 43 were recaptured and since then no [SOURCE: Page_207]

more stocking occurred. From 1993 to 2001, pikeperch numbers grew and then slightly decreased [SOURCE: Page_207]

in 2002. [SOURCE: Page_207]

Thus for more than 50 years a pikeperch population has been present in the Vaccarès Lagoon and [SOURCE: Page_207]

in the Fumemorte Canal where environmental conditions and population dynamics were likely [SOURCE: Page_207]

much different from those found in the Rhône River. Indeed the fluctuation of the environmental [SOURCE: Page_207]

conditions and the size of the population in the Vaccarès-Fumemorte system made the population [SOURCE: Page_207]

dynamics more chaotic than in the Rhône River. So the Vaccarès-Fumemorte population could [SOURCE: Page_207]

have genetically diverged from the Rhône population through both demographic processes [SOURCE: Page_207]

(genetic drift) and selective processes (natural selection). This divergence had probably been [SOURCE: Page_207]

enforced by the stocking events. Even though no reliable interpretations have been done [SOURCE: Page_207]

concerning the Rhône and the Vaccarès due to the small size of this samples, this is confirmed by [SOURCE: Page_207]

i) the significant Fis (0.15) found for the Camargue population (i.e. the four samples pooled) that [SOURCE: Page_207]

would rather tend to indicate a structuring of the Rhône delta population, ii) the significant Fst [SOURCE: Page_207]

(0.04) between the Fumemorte and the Bouic population and iii) the significant differences in [SOURCE: Page_207]

LDH-C$_1$* patterns between the Fumemorte and the Bouic populations, but not between the [SOURCE: Page_207]

Rhône River and the Bouic populations. So none or very few migrations occur between the Bouic [SOURCE: Page_207]

17 [SOURCE: Page_207]

and the Fumemorte canals but as no significant differences were observed in LDH-C$_1$* patterns [SOURCE: Page_208]

migration may exist between the Rhône River and the Bouic canal through the pumping stations. [SOURCE: Page_208]

The causes of morphological differences between populations are often quite difficult to explain [SOURCE: Page_208]

(CADRIN 2000). Phenotype is under the double control of environmental conditions and genotype [SOURCE: Page_208]

(e.g. (HARD et al. 1999), but morphological changes can be rapid when new different [SOURCE: Page_208]

environmental conditions occurred (KLEPAKER 1993). The Bouic Canal undergoes large water [SOURCE: Page_208]

level fluctuations and great parts of it remain dry during most of the agriculture growing season. [SOURCE: Page_208]

Furthermore, prey density is much lower than in the Fumemorte Canal (unpublished data) that is [SOURCE: Page_208]

inhabited by a large sandsmelt (Atherina boyeri R.) population (ROSECCHI & CRIVELLI 1995). [SOURCE: Page_208]

Thus, Fumemorte is more suitable for pikeperch growth than the Bouic Canal. The growth rate is [SOURCE: Page_208]

also often involved in the variation of otolith shape (CAMPANA & CASSELMAN 1993). [SOURCE: Page_208]

Nevertheless, whatever the determinism of the phenotypic variation, the biometric results confirm [SOURCE: Page_208]

the genetic results supporting the isolation of the Fumemorte population. [SOURCE: Page_208]

Isolation of the Camargue and consequence on the population dynamics [SOURCE: Page_208]

The isolation of the pikeperch population inhabiting the Fumemorte Canal would mean that the [SOURCE: Page_208]

number of migrants from the Bouic Canal is quite low or nul. Nevertheless, passage between the [SOURCE: Page_208]

two canals remains possible since (CRIVELLI & POIZAT 2001) describe the downstream migration [SOURCE: Page_208]

of 0+ shad (Alosa fallax rhodanensis R.) from the Rhône to the Vaccarès Lagoon via the [SOURCE: Page_208]

pumping stations, the Bouic and the Fumemorte Canal. The shad may use the marshes to pass [SOURCE: Page_208]

from the irrigation to the drainage system although, as for pikeperch, no 0+ shad were captured in [SOURCE: Page_208]

the marshes (POIZAT & CRIVELLI 1997). The presence of 0+ shad in the drainage canal can be [SOURCE: Page_208]

explained by the migratory behaviour of the shad (anadromous species). Furthermore, the number [SOURCE: Page_208]

of 0+ pikeperch pumped from the Rhône is lower than the number of 0+ shad (POIZAT et al. [SOURCE: Page_208]

18 [SOURCE: Page_208]

1999). Typical riverine species like nase (Chondrostoma nasus L.), chub (Leuciscus cephalus L.) [SOURCE: Page_209]

or common barbel (Barbus barbus L.) are also pumped from the Rhône river in greater numbers [SOURCE: Page_209]

than shad (POIZAT et al. 1999), whereas few are captured in the drainage canal in which they do [SOURCE: Page_209]

not reproduce (unpublished data). So it seems that the degree of isolation of a population [SOURCE: Page_209]

depends on species behaviour. This is congruent with the results of (TIBBETS & DOWLING 1996) [SOURCE: Page_209]

showing that the degree of isolation between populations is affected by the intrinsic [SOURCE: Page_209]

characteristics such as dispersal capabilities and the reproductive behaviour of the species. [SOURCE: Page_209]

If fish can travel from the Rhône to the Vaccarès Lagoon, they cannot go back. If a species is [SOURCE: Page_209]

reproductively successful in both compartments (i.e. Rhône and Camargue), with a limited [SOURCE: Page_209]

number of migrants per generation, this generates a particular case of metapopulation: both [SOURCE: Page_209]

subpopulations are spatially separated but linked unidirectionally. This could be the case for the [SOURCE: Page_209]

pikeperch if the salinity in the Vaccarès Lagoon decreased to 5 g/L. There, it would find optimal [SOURCE: Page_209]

conditions to reproduce. If the subpopulation receiving migrants has a low or a null reproduction, [SOURCE: Page_209]

it is a case of a source-sink model. This could hold for the pikeperch because recruitment in the [SOURCE: Page_209]

drainage canal (and in the irrigation canal also) can fluctuate greatly (unpublished data). Thus, [SOURCE: Page_209]

source-sink and metapopulation processes probably occur together or alternatively. [SOURCE: Page_209]

Whatever the actual situation, pikeperch conservation in the Fumemorte Canal seems to mainly [SOURCE: Page_209]

depend on the recruitment of this population rather than on the migrants from the Rhône River [SOURCE: Page_209]

(via the Bouic Canal). The drainage canal is submitted to great human pressure that can be very [SOURCE: Page_209]

damageable for several aquatic species. For instance every year, a large quantity of pesticide [SOURCE: Page_209]

sprayed on rice fields enters the Fumemorte Canal. Residents said that in the past, accidents with [SOURCE: Page_209]

algicides caused massive fish mortality. If the Vaccarès Lagoon remains unsafe for pikeperch, the [SOURCE: Page_209]

limited number of migrants from the Rhône may not be sufficient to maintain the pikeperch [SOURCE: Page_209]

19 [SOURCE: Page_209]

population in the case of a pesticide accident. It may need a longer time for the Rhône population [SOURCE: Page_210]

to re-colonise the Camargue. [SOURCE: Page_210]

We showed that the genetic variation observed in the Camargue population can just as well [SOURCE: Page_210]

involve demographic than selective processes. It would be interesting to test if microsatellites [SOURCE: Page_210]

(WIRTH et al. 1999), known to give a close estimation of the heterozygosity at the large-scale [SOURCE: Page_210]

level than allozymic markers (NEFF & GROSS 2001), are able to give such a close estimation at a [SOURCE: Page_210]

more restrictive-scale. Moreover, investigations with microsatellites, which provide the powerful [SOURCE: Page_210]

degree of inter-individual discrimination, should allow to quantify the number of pikeperch (Nm) [SOURCE: Page_210]

passing from the Rhône River into the Camargue, its diked delta and to debate more precisely the [SOURCE: Page_210]

demographic versus selective hypotheses propose in the present paper. [SOURCE: Page_210]

## ANNEXE 2

Annexe 2 Poulet N, Arzel C, Messad S, Lek, S and Argillier, C. Diel activity of adult pikeperch Sander lucioperca (L.) in a drainage canal in the Mediterranean basin during spring. Hydrobiologia. Sous presse. [SOURCE: Page_231]

227 [SOURCE: Page_231]

228 [SOURCE: Page_232]

Diel activity of adult pikeperch Sander lucioperca (L.) in a [SOURCE: Page_233]

drainage canal in the Mediterranean basin during spring [SOURCE: Page_233]

Nicolas Poulet^1, Céline Arzel^1, Samir Messad^2, Sovan Lek^3 & Christine Argillier^1 [SOURCE: Page_233]

1 Fish Resources Management Research Group, Cemagref, 361, Rue JF Breton, BP 5095, [SOURCE: Page_233]

Montpellier Cedex 01 34033, France. 2 CIRAD-EMVT, Campus international de Baillarguet, BP 5035, 34032 Montpellier Cedex 1, [SOURCE: Page_233]

France. 3 LADYBIO (CNRS-UPS) Laboratoire Dynamique de la Biodiversité, Université Paul [SOURCE: Page_233]

Sabatier, 118 route de Narbonne 31062 Toulouse cedex 4, France. [SOURCE: Page_233]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_233]

Pikeperch (Sander lucioperca) is a broadly distributed fish species in Europe but little is [SOURCE: Page_233]

known about its ecology in the southern part of its distribution area in warm climatic [SOURCE: Page_233]

conditions. The aim of this study was to analyse pikeperch rate of movement and to assess [SOURCE: Page_233]

whether it displayed a diel pattern related to temperature. Thus acoustic telemetry was used to [SOURCE: Page_233]

track adult pikeperch in a drainage canal located in south of France. The survey was carried [SOURCE: Page_233]

out in spring, during the spawning period. [SOURCE: Page_233]

The results showed that females were more active than males. This is in accordance with [SOURCE: Page_233]

previous data on the nest guardian behaviour of the males. For both genders, the activity rates [SOURCE: Page_233]

increased during the study period as water temperature rose. Males and females displayed the [SOURCE: Page_233]

same diel activity with a maximum at dusk, thereby confirming many indirect observations. [SOURCE: Page_233]

Nevertheless, inter-individuals variations were observed. Thus, these results on diel activity [SOURCE: Page_233]

are rather a general trend than a strict rule and suggest the involvement of other factors than [SOURCE: Page_234]

light intensity in the control of diel activity. This diel rhythm is positively correlated to water [SOURCE: Page_234]

temperature for females. Pikeperch activity may be the result of a trade-off between [SOURCE: Page_234]

physiological requirements of temperature and light, satisfaction of energy needs and [SOURCE: Page_234]

avoidance of predators. [SOURCE: Page_234]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_234]

The pikeperch (Sander lucioperca L.) is a large piscivorous Percid fish broadly distributed in [SOURCE: Page_234]

Europe. Its range extends from the Caspian Sea to the Iberian Peninsula and from the [SOURCE: Page_234]

Scandinavian waters to the Mediterranean basin, as well as in the United Kingdom. [SOURCE: Page_234]

Nevertheless, most of the information about its ecology and its demography comes from the [SOURCE: Page_234]

northern and the eastern parts of its distribution area (e.g. Dahl, 1984; Nyberg et al., 1996) [SOURCE: Page_234]

where it has an important commercial and recreational value. [SOURCE: Page_234]

Previous studies using mark-recapture methods (Goubier, 1975; Fickling et al., 1985) have [SOURCE: Page_234]

shown that pikeperch could travel over relatively long distances (10-30 km and exceptionally [SOURCE: Page_234]

> 200 km). Some of these movements correspond to seasonal migrations associated with [SOURCE: Page_234]

stages of its life cycle. In early spring, adults migrate to reach spawning areas (Lehtonen, [SOURCE: Page_234]

1983; Lehtonen et al., 1988; Koed et al., 2000) which are used every year, revealing a [SOURCE: Page_234]

spawning site fidelity (Puke, 1952; Lehtonen et al., 1988; Jepsen et al., 1999). In autumn, [SOURCE: Page_234]

when water temperature decreases, pikeperch migrate to deeper overwintering areas (Lind, [SOURCE: Page_234]

1977; Lehtonen, 1983). Furthermore, some of these migrations (over 10 km) were attributed [SOURCE: Page_234]

to the search for prey fish (Lind, 1977; Lehtonen, 1983; Fickling et al., 1985; Koed et al., [SOURCE: Page_234]

2000). Although the migratory behaviour of the pikeperch is generally well understood, its [SOURCE: Page_234]

diel activity pattern is still poorly documented. Pikeperch is a predator considered as active [SOURCE: Page_234]

during crepuscular and nocturnal periods. This was deduced from physiological [SOURCE: Page_234]

considerations (Ali et al., 1977) or from other indirect observations (i.e. diet analysis see [SOURCE: Page_234]

Fedorova et al., 1982). Indeed, the pikeperch's retina possesses a tapetum lucidum (layer of [SOURCE: Page_235]

cells reflecting and intensifying light) that enables it to navigate in turbid waters an low light [SOURCE: Page_235]

conditions (Ali et al., 1977). Studies of the stomach contents at different periods of the day [SOURCE: Page_235]

have shown that pikeperch exhibited crepuscular feeding patterns (Fedorova et al., 1982). [SOURCE: Page_235]

Various anecdotal observations made by anglers generally support these results (Linfield et [SOURCE: Page_235]

al., 1979; Pollet, 1959) but some high capture rates have also been reported in summer during [SOURCE: Page_235]

periods when the daily light intensity was highest (Tarragnat, 2001). [SOURCE: Page_235]

However, this is all based on indirect evidence (i.e. tagging, diet contents, angling). To our [SOURCE: Page_235]

knowledge, no studies using techniques providing direct evidence and aiming at describing [SOURCE: Page_235]

the diel activity of pikeperch have been conducted or published. Using radio-telemetry, [SOURCE: Page_235]

Jepsen et al. (1999) localised pikeperch every 6 hours, a frequency that is suffisant neither to [SOURCE: Page_235]

highlight a diel pattern nor to link it to environmental factors (Baras, 1998; Ovidio et al., [SOURCE: Page_235]

2000). [SOURCE: Page_235]

Our study lasted from March to June 2002 (i.e. during the spawning period) in a drainage [SOURCE: Page_235]

canal located in the southern part of the pikeperch distribution area. We used acoustic [SOURCE: Page_235]

telemetry to investigate the activity of adult pikeperch during the spawning period. The aim of [SOURCE: Page_235]

the present paper was i) to assess whether pikeperch gross rate of movement varied among [SOURCE: Page_235]

month or gender and ii) to assess whether the pikeperch displayed a diel activity rhythm and if [SOURCE: Page_235]

so if this rhythm could be related to temperature conditions. [SOURCE: Page_235]

Material and methods [SOURCE: Page_235]

Study site [SOURCE: Page_235]

The study site was located in the Rhône delta, southern France (43°34 N, 4°34 E) (Fig. 1). [SOURCE: Page_235]

The Fumemorte canal collects water from a complex canal network spreading over 68 km² [SOURCE: Page_235]

(Chauvelon, 1998). The main channel is 14.6 km long but if the largest tributaries are [SOURCE: Page_235]

included, it forms a 32.5 km canal network (Fig. 1). The canal is shallow (1 m) and around 14 [SOURCE: Page_236]

m wide. Its drains freshwater from marshes and rice fields and flows into the Vaccarès [SOURCE: Page_236]

lagoon. At its outlet, the Fumemorte canal has a raisable barrier designed to limit entrance of [SOURCE: Page_236]

salt water. The barrier being submerged; most of the mobile aquatic organisms can move [SOURCE: Page_236]

between the canal and the lagoon (Rosecchi et al., 1995). The Vaccarès lagoon is brackish [SOURCE: Page_236]

(salinity > 10 ppt), and the salinity in the Fumemorte canal varies from 0.1 ppt in summer to 5 [SOURCE: Page_236]

ppt in winter depending on the agricultural activities and on the strength and direction of the [SOURCE: Page_236]

wind. The water remains highly turbid throughout the year (Secchi depth < 40 cm). The mean [SOURCE: Page_236]

monthly water temperature ranges from 6.5°C in January (with minima at 1.0°C) to 23.5°C in [SOURCE: Page_236]

July (with maximuma up to 27°C). A more detailed description of the study site is provided in [SOURCE: Page_236]

Poulet et al. (2004). [SOURCE: Page_236]

Pikeperch is the most abundant piscivorous fish in the Fumemorte canal. The fish community [SOURCE: Page_236]

is mainly composed of topmouth gudgeon (Pseudorasbora parva L.), bleak (Alburnus [SOURCE: Page_236]

alburnus L.), sand smelt (Atherina boyeri R.), eel (Anguilla anguilla L.), crucian carp [SOURCE: Page_236]

(Carassius gibelio L.), common carp (Cyprinus carpio L.), pumpkinseed (Lepomis gibbosus [SOURCE: Page_236]

L.), mullet (Mugil cephalus and Liza spp), rudd (Scardinius erythrophthalmus L.), black [SOURCE: Page_236]

bullhead (Ameiurus melas L.), wells catfish (Silurus glanis L.) and pike (Esox lucius L.). A [SOURCE: Page_236]

total of 34 species occurs in the Fumemorte basin (Crivelli, com. Pers.). [SOURCE: Page_236]

Capture and tagging [SOURCE: Page_236]

Thirteen pikeperch were captured in the Fumemorte canal (Table 1) with fyke nets (mesh size [SOURCE: Page_236]

6 mm) and gill nets (mesh size 40, 55 and 80 mm). Most of fish were captured from late [SOURCE: Page_236]

January to early March, except three in late March and one in April. Only the latter had [SOURCE: Page_236]

spawned. At this period of the year, external features allow a visual determination of the [SOURCE: Page_236]

gender: males exhibit a dark abdomen while that of females is white and bigger (Deelder et [SOURCE: Page_236]

al., 1964). [SOURCE: Page_236]

Because of the high water conductivity (>1000 µS/cm), we used acoustic transmitters (Lotek [SOURCE: Page_237]

CAFT 11_3, 40 mm x 11 mm, 9.2g in air, 4.5g in water) identifiable by individual code and [SOURCE: Page_237]

operated at 76.8 kHz. Their life span was theoretically at least 172 days with a pulse rate of 1 [SOURCE: Page_237]

beat every 5 seconds. [SOURCE: Page_237]

Fish were anaesthetised in a bath containing 5ml.l-1 of 2-phenoxyethanol, measured (fork [SOURCE: Page_237]

length) and weighed. Internal implantation of transmitters was preferred to external [SOURCE: Page_237]

attachment because Koed & Thorstad (2001) had shown that it did not influence the [SOURCE: Page_237]

swimming speed of pikeperch and the method had been employed in previous studies on [SOURCE: Page_237]

pikeperch without any trouble (Koed et al., 2001; Koed et al., 2000; Jepsen et al., 1999). The [SOURCE: Page_237]

anaesthetised fish were placed in a V-shaped support. The transmitters were disinfected [SOURCE: Page_237]

(Hibitan) and then surgically implanted into the body cavity through a ventral 20 mm incision [SOURCE: Page_237]

posterior to the pelvic girdle. Antibiotic (Duphapen LA, 0.1 ml.kg-1) was injected in the body [SOURCE: Page_237]

cavity to prevent post-surgery infections. The incision was closed with three independent [SOURCE: Page_237]

sutures (Ethicon Vicryl 2/0). These sutures were absorbable, but should have lasted for about [SOURCE: Page_237]

one month. Fish were also marked with coloured spots on the pelvic girdle using Alcian blue [SOURCE: Page_237]

injected with a dermojet. This enabled anglers to identify and release them in case of capture. [SOURCE: Page_237]

Each operation took about 5 min and the fish were returned to an oxygenated recovery tank. [SOURCE: Page_237]

Recovery time was about 5 to 10 min but fish were maintained one night in observation and [SOURCE: Page_237]

released the following morning near their capture point. [SOURCE: Page_237]

Tracking [SOURCE: Page_237]

The Fish were tracked from boat using a directional hydrophone connected to a receiver [SOURCE: Page_237]

(Lotek model SRX_400). Their locations were determined according to the signal strength. [SOURCE: Page_237]

When a signal was first received, the motor was turned off to approach as quietly as possible. [SOURCE: Page_237]

Tracking session took place monthly from March to June over 24h with positions recorded [SOURCE: Page_237]

every two hours beginning at 06.00 h. The time reference was according to the Universal [SOURCE: Page_237]

Time Coordinated (UTC) that also corresponds to the Greenwich Mean Time (GMT). The [SOURCE: Page_238]

positions were recorded using a GPS receiver. Due to the length of the canal and the number [SOURCE: Page_238]

of tracked individuals, tracking all of them in a single session would not have allowed a two [SOURCE: Page_238]

hours time lapse between consecutive locations. Thus, a preliminary survey was carried out [SOURCE: Page_238]

the day before each session in order to choose the individuals to be tracked. Only those within [SOURCE: Page_238]

a 5 km reach were tracked the next day (even if they left this perimeter). A second 24h session [SOURCE: Page_238]

was performed one week later (other tracking experiments for different purpose occurred in [SOURCE: Page_238]

this lapse of time) to track the remaining fish. Those fish that had not been tracked by the end [SOURCE: Page_238]

of the second session were considered missing for the month. Thus the selection of the [SOURCE: Page_238]

tracked fish did not depend either on their activity or the easiness of positioning but on their [SOURCE: Page_238]

position the day before the session. So there was no bias leading to an underestimation of the [SOURCE: Page_238]

fish movements (Gowan et al., 1994). [SOURCE: Page_238]

Analysis [SOURCE: Page_238]

We used Mapinfo 6.5 (Mapinfo, 2000) to report fish position on a digitised map of the [SOURCE: Page_238]

Fumemorte basin. The distance covered by a fish between two consecutive positions (i.e. 2 [SOURCE: Page_238]

hours interval) was assessed. This distance represented the minimal distance covered since we [SOURCE: Page_238]

did not know if the fish swam in a straight-line. Then, the rate of movement was calculated by [SOURCE: Page_238]

dividing this minimal distance by the time separating the two consecutive fish positions and [SOURCE: Page_238]

expressed in m.h-1. Although this method clearly underestimates fish activity, it does provide [SOURCE: Page_238]

some relevant insights into the patterns of gross activity (Cooke et al., 2001). [SOURCE: Page_238]

First of all, Pearson correlations were used to assess the relationship between mean rate [SOURCE: Page_238]

activity and fish length. [SOURCE: Page_238]

The distances travelled in 24 hours (i.e. for a given fish, the sum of the distances measured [SOURCE: Page_238]

between the successive positions), daily ranges (i.e. for a given fish, the distance between the [SOURCE: Page_238]

most upstream and the most downstream positions) and rates of movement were then [SOURCE: Page_238]

compared between months and between genders using non-parametric tests (Mann-Whitney [SOURCE: Page_239]

U and Kruskal-Wallis tests). Diel patterns were studied graphically using Lowess fitting. In [SOURCE: Page_239]

order to assess the link between activity and temperature, Spearman correlations were [SOURCE: Page_239]

performed between mean rates of movement by gender and by 2h interval and mean [SOURCE: Page_239]

temperature by 2h interval. [SOURCE: Page_239]

Results [SOURCE: Page_239]

Tracking and survival of tagged fish [SOURCE: Page_239]

Thirteen fish were tagged (Table 1) during this experiment. Three individuals (N°46, 156 & [SOURCE: Page_239]

162) disappeared before the beginning of the tracking experience. Transmitter N°46 was [SOURCE: Page_239]

found in the water beneath a great cormorant (Phalacrocorax carbo) resting place located on [SOURCE: Page_239]

the canal bank. Fish N°156 and 162 were never found. Pikeperch N°143 was lost in late April. [SOURCE: Page_239]

Another pikeperch (N°170) was captured by an angler in June. Fish N°110 became stationary [SOURCE: Page_239]

from mid April until the battery ran out. Attempts to make it move failed: it could have died [SOURCE: Page_239]

or lost its transmitter. As the transmitter was in woody debris, we were unable to recuperate it. [SOURCE: Page_239]

The battery of N°73 ran out of power during the June session. [SOURCE: Page_239]

All the fish have not been tracked each month and/or in a same 24h session. Indeed, the canal [SOURCE: Page_239]

network was very large and pikeperch did not necessarily remain in the main canal (Poulet, [SOURCE: Page_239]

unpublished data). Thus in order to position fish every two hours, a maximum of four fishes [SOURCE: Page_239]

per 24h has been tracked. [SOURCE: Page_239]

Pikeperch N°74 was recaptured on 10th July: it had a 516mm fork length for 1370g that is a [SOURCE: Page_239]

growth of 64mm and 449g since its initial capture (20th of February). The scar due to [SOURCE: Page_239]

transmitter implantation was completely healed and hardly visible. Pikeperch N°96 was [SOURCE: Page_239]

caught by an angler on late May 2004. Its size at this time was about 800mm i.e. the double of [SOURCE: Page_239]

its size at the tagging time, a year and a half earlier. The other individuals were not recaptured [SOURCE: Page_239]

either because their transmitters were running out of power or because they were located in [SOURCE: Page_240]

private parts of the canal where we did not have fishing authorisation. [SOURCE: Page_240]

Activity [SOURCE: Page_240]

There was no correlation between the mean daily rate of movement (m.h-1) and the length of [SOURCE: Page_240]

pikeperch either for males (Pearson correlation, R=-0.41, p=0.585, n=4) or for females [SOURCE: Page_240]

(Pearson correlation, R=0.79, p=0.11, n=5) or both genders combined (Pearson correlation, [SOURCE: Page_240]

R=0.3, p=0.43, n=9). Thus, all statistical analyses were performed on all the individuals, [SOURCE: Page_240]

regardless of the size. [SOURCE: Page_240]

The three highest daily ranges were 3410m, 2917m and 2394m performed by three different [SOURCE: Page_240]

females. For all months pooled, the daily range was larger for females than for males (Mann- [SOURCE: Page_240]

Whitney test, U=26.5, p=0.007). Although the mean daily range was shorter in March (Table [SOURCE: Page_240]

2), there was no difference between months regardless of the gender (Kruskal-Wallis Test, [SOURCE: Page_240]

p=0.460). [SOURCE: Page_240]

The three highest distances travelled in 24 hours were 7561m, 5284m and 4945m, [SOURCE: Page_240]

corresponding to the three females having the largest daily home ranges. Fifty-four percent of [SOURCE: Page_240]

the daily distances travelled were over 1000m and 33% over 2000m. [SOURCE: Page_240]

Both daily range and distance travelled displayed high standard variation suggesting an [SOURCE: Page_240]

important individual variability (Table 2). [SOURCE: Page_240]

The highest rate of movement recorded in a 2-hour interval was 2640m.h-1 moved by a female [SOURCE: Page_240]

in March. This speed represents 1.62 body lengths per second (bl.s-1). All the other rates of [SOURCE: Page_240]

movement recorded were below 775m.h-1. The highest movement was an outlier and therefore [SOURCE: Page_240]

excluded from further analysis. During the study, the daily mean water temperature increased [SOURCE: Page_240]

from 8.6°C to 25.7°C (Fig. 2). The males significantly increased their rate of movement from [SOURCE: Page_240]

Marsh to June whereas females displayed more constant rate apart for June (Table 3 & Fig. [SOURCE: Page_240]

3). Within each month, males exhibited a lower rate of movement than females except in June [SOURCE: Page_241]

(Table 4 & Fig. 3). [SOURCE: Page_241]

Diel pattern [SOURCE: Page_241]

An important individual variability in the diel pattern was observed (Fig. 4). For example, fish [SOURCE: Page_241]

N°86 moved preferentially in the morning whereas N°74 and 128 were mostly active at dusk. [SOURCE: Page_241]

All of the males have remained still during at least one whole session. Several individuals, [SOURCE: Page_241]

both males and females, displayed similar diel patterns among months when they were [SOURCE: Page_241]

moving (N°73, 74, 86). Conversely, pikeperch N°50, for example, displayed diurnal activity [SOURCE: Page_241]

in April and May but later shifted to activity peaks in the morning and early night. [SOURCE: Page_241]

Water temperature was significantly correlated to the diel activity for female (n=11, R=0.62, [SOURCE: Page_241]

P=0.04) but not for males (n=11, R=0.28, P=0.52) (Fig. 5). [SOURCE: Page_241]

Discussion [SOURCE: Page_241]

Tracking protocol [SOURCE: Page_241]

From the 13 fish tagged initially, nine were successfully tracked. One of these pikeperch has [SOURCE: Page_241]

probably been predated by a great cormorant, which is not surprisingly since it is able to [SOURCE: Page_241]

swallow an adult pikeperch (Santoul et al., 2004). Indeed, many of our marked pikeperch [SOURCE: Page_241]

displayed scars obviously caused by these birds. The fate of the three other missing tagged [SOURCE: Page_241]

fish remained unknown. During and after the study, two pikeperch were caught by anglers [SOURCE: Page_241]

and one by a fisherman: this gives some indications on the predators of pikeperch in the [SOURCE: Page_241]

Fumemorte canal and on the disappearance of the other tagged fishes. [SOURCE: Page_241]

In our study, the tag to body mass ratios were close to the values of former successful studies [SOURCE: Page_241]

on this species which did not show any trouble (Jepsen et al., 1999; Jepsen, 2003; Koed et al., [SOURCE: Page_241]

2001). The two individuals recaptured after 4 and 29 months displayed a growth in [SOURCE: Page_241]

accordance with previous results for this population (Poulet et al., 2004) which confirms that [SOURCE: Page_242]

pikeperch have well bear the transmitter implantation. [SOURCE: Page_242]

General behaviour [SOURCE: Page_242]

In the Fumemorte canal, pikeperch can move over quite long distances in 24 hours: the [SOURCE: Page_242]

majority of the observations were over 1 km with a maximum of 7.5 km. Comparison with the [SOURCE: Page_242]

daily distance travelled in spring and in similar water temperature by female shows it is higher [SOURCE: Page_242]

(over 3.5 km) in the Fumemorte canal than in the Bygholm reservoir (Jepsen et al., 1999) or [SOURCE: Page_242]

in the River Gudenaa (Koed et al., 2000) (both 2 km) in Denmark. This could reflect a [SOURCE: Page_242]

difference in the tracking protocol. Precision of positioning differed according the method and [SOURCE: Page_242]

the frequency of positioning could have a major influence on the estimation of the distance [SOURCE: Page_242]

travelled (Baras, 1998; Ovidio et al., 2000). When calculating the daily distance travelled on a [SOURCE: Page_242]

6-hour interval basis, we found that females travel on average 2.7 km which is not so different [SOURCE: Page_242]

from the results of Jepsen et al. (1999) and Koed et al. (2000). Overall, our results suggest [SOURCE: Page_242]

that the pikeperch is able to exploit a large part of the Fumemorte network. [SOURCE: Page_242]

Although pikeperch is a slow swimmer like the majority of Percids (Peake et al., 2000; Craig, [SOURCE: Page_242]

2000) we observed some fast movement (1.6 bl.s-1). On a 6h-interval basis, our fastest [SOURCE: Page_242]

movement becomes 0.28 bl.s-1 and the second fastest movement, 0.16 bl.s-1 which is in the [SOURCE: Page_242]

same range than the results of Jepsen et al. (1999). With an interval of 0.5h, we might have [SOURCE: Page_242]

found values as high as those found by Kelso (1976) for walleye (i.e. up to 3 bl.s-1). [SOURCE: Page_242]

Unlike in Jepsen et al. (1999), neither the mean rate of movements nor the mean daily range [SOURCE: Page_242]

were significantly correlated to fish length. However, as far as females are concerned, the [SOURCE: Page_242]

relationship between length and rate of movement displayed a high (R=0.79) but not [SOURCE: Page_242]

significant correlation coefficient due to the low size of the sample (n=5). Thus the absence of [SOURCE: Page_242]

significant correlation on the whole sample can be explained by the fact that our study was [SOURCE: Page_242]

performed during the spawning period when males are less active whatever their size. [SOURCE: Page_242]

Spring activity [SOURCE: Page_243]

In March and April, males displayed a significantly lower rate of movement and daily range [SOURCE: Page_243]

than females. This is consistent with the spawning behaviour previously described (Jepsen et [SOURCE: Page_243]

al., 1999; Koed et al., 2000). The male prepares the nest and takes care of the eggs and the [SOURCE: Page_243]

young fry (Sonesten, 1991; Craig, 2000). In this nest guarding species, although the male is [SOURCE: Page_243]

less mobile, it also spends energy since parental care is costly (Cooke et al., 2002). Indeed, the [SOURCE: Page_243]

males have less opportunity to feed when guarding the nest (Goncalves et al., 1997). On the [SOURCE: Page_243]

contrary, the females spend very little time on the nest (Erm, 1981; Lappalainen et al., 2003) [SOURCE: Page_243]

but invest much energy in vitellogenesis since the species displays a high fecundity (about [SOURCE: Page_243]

200 000 eggs per kg). Consequently, high female activities during the spawning period could [SOURCE: Page_243]

respond to an increase in the food needs (Schlumberger et al., 1996; Jepsen et al., 2000) in [SOURCE: Page_243]

order to maintain energetic reserves and ensure metabolic functions. [SOURCE: Page_243]

The activity of both genders increased from March to May as the water temperature rose and [SOURCE: Page_243]

day length increased. Indeed, water temperature in the Fumemorte canal had still not reached [SOURCE: Page_243]

27°C, i.e. the physiological optimum temperature for pikeperch (Hokanson, 1977) by the end [SOURCE: Page_243]

of the study in May. Therefore, we could assume that the activity will increase until the water [SOURCE: Page_243]

temperature is around 27°C. This positive relationship between temperature and pikeperch [SOURCE: Page_243]

activity is consistent with previous results obtained on an annual scale (Jepsen et al, 1999; [SOURCE: Page_243]

Koed et al, 2000). Nevertheless, rate of movement seemed to stabilise or even decrease in [SOURCE: Page_243]

June. This suggests that other factors (e.g. dissolved oxygen) influence the activity before [SOURCE: Page_243]

temperature reach pikeperch optimum. Nevertheless, too few fish have been tracked in June to [SOURCE: Page_243]

be affirmative. [SOURCE: Page_243]

Diel activity [SOURCE: Page_243]

Pikeperch displayed a diel activity rhythm in the Fumemorte canal during spring. [SOURCE: Page_243]

Nevertheless this diel activity was more obvious for females than for males, most of them [SOURCE: Page_243]

being stationary on the nests. Our direct observations showed that during the spawning period [SOURCE: Page_244]

pikeperch were mainly crepuscular, which is consistent with its status of low-light hunter (Ali [SOURCE: Page_244]

et al., 1977) and confirmed the many indirect observations suggesting activity peaks during [SOURCE: Page_244]

the dark hours (Fedorova et al., 1982; Craig, 2000). We also observed inter-individual [SOURCE: Page_244]

variations: some pikeperch were rather diurnal, which has already been observed (Jepsen et [SOURCE: Page_244]

al., 1999; Tarragnat, 2001). Hence, the maximum activity at dusk is rather a general trend [SOURCE: Page_244]

than a strict rule. These results suggest the involvement of other factors than light intensity in [SOURCE: Page_244]

the control of the activity rates. We showed that temperature was an important factor driving [SOURCE: Page_244]

female diel activity. Water temperature plays a major role in swimming speed of fish [SOURCE: Page_244]

(Hergenrader et al., 1967) and as the water temperature in spring was below the pikeperch [SOURCE: Page_244]

physiological optimum, perhaps this explains why pikeperch moved more during the warmest [SOURCE: Page_244]

hours. Like many predators, pikeperch may adapt its behaviour to increase the probability of [SOURCE: Page_244]

meeting its prey (e.g. Jepsen et al., 2000). The peak of water temperature occurs at dusk and [SOURCE: Page_244]

most fish activity depends on temperature. Fish behaviour may also depend on predator [SOURCE: Page_244]

avoidance (Metcalfe et al., 1999). The Rhône delta is an important wintering or nesting area [SOURCE: Page_244]

for many piscivorous birds and this may influence fish behaviour (e.g. Allouche et al., 2001). [SOURCE: Page_244]

As the Great Cormorant is diurnal (Cramp et al., 1980), it could be less risky for pikeperch to [SOURCE: Page_244]

move during the dark hours. [SOURCE: Page_244]

To conclude, in the Fumemorte canal, the rate of movement increased from March to May, [SOURCE: Page_244]

with the females being more active than the males, travelling over quite long distances in 24 [SOURCE: Page_244]

hours. Both sexes displayed the same diel activity pattern between months with a maximum [SOURCE: Page_244]

activity at dusk and a minimum at dawn. The diel rhythm of pikeperch may be driven by the [SOURCE: Page_244]

trade-off between its physiological requirements of temperature and light, satisfying its [SOURCE: Page_244]

energy needs and avoiding predators. The influence of these factors in the overall behavioural [SOURCE: Page_244]

patterns of pikeperch could be assessed with telemetry experiment performed in controlled [SOURCE: Page_244]

conditions (e.g. with or without predator) and using electromyogram (EMG) transmitters to [SOURCE: Page_245]

quantify the activity (Cooke et al., 2004). [SOURCE: Page_245]

## 15                                           ABSTRACT

16 [SOURCE: Page_260]

17 [SOURCE: Page_260]

18   This study focused on the habitat preferences of the pikeperch in a drainage canal connected to [SOURCE: Page_260]

19   a brackish lagoon located in the Rhône delta, France (a southern area of pikeperch [SOURCE: Page_260]

20   distribution). Thirteen pikeperch (Sander lucioperca) were implanted with acoustic [SOURCE: Page_260]

21   transmitters and ten were successfully tracked twice a week from March to early July. [SOURCE: Page_260]

22   Pikeperch was found in the main canal and in narrow tributaries, but never in the lagoon. [SOURCE: Page_260]

23   Pikeperch avoided shallow parts of the canal and selected areas with high riparian vegetation [SOURCE: Page_260]

24   (mainly trees). No between sexes differences occurred in habitat selection. At the network [SOURCE: Page_260]

25   scale, while both sexes remained in the upstream part of the canal during the spawning period, [SOURCE: Page_260]

26   after spawning, females migrated downstream and also into the tributaries. This migration may [SOURCE: Page_260]

27   be induced by the movement of prey while males remained guarding the nests. This study [SOURCE: Page_260]

28   provides guidelines for increasing habitat suitability within artificial hydrosystems, particularly [SOURCE: Page_260]

29   by the development of riparian vegetation. [SOURCE: Page_260]

30 [SOURCE: Page_260]

31 [SOURCE: Page_260]

32 [SOURCE: Page_260]

33 [SOURCE: Page_260]

34   Keywords: Habitat use; Sander lucioperca; canal; predator; Rhône Delta. [SOURCE: Page_260]

35 [SOURCE: Page_260]

2 [SOURCE: Page_260]

## 36                                      INTRODUCTION

37 [SOURCE: Page_261]

38 [SOURCE: Page_261]

39   At the beginning of the 1990’s, irrigated lands occupied about 250 million hectares worldwide [SOURCE: Page_261]

40   (Anonymous, 2003). In spite of this importance, few studies have analysed the role of [SOURCE: Page_261]

41   irrigation canals and drainages as fishways, permanent or temporary habitats for freshwater or [SOURCE: Page_261]

42   diadromous fish (Townsend & Peirson, 1988). [SOURCE: Page_261]

43 [SOURCE: Page_261]

44 [SOURCE: Page_261]

45   Most artificial habitats, especially canals, lack the riffle-pool sequences that are typical of [SOURCE: Page_261]

46   rivers, their slope is low or null and their substratum is generally homogenous and comprises [SOURCE: Page_261]

47   essentially fine sediments (mainly silt and clay). These traits make irrigation/drainage canals [SOURCE: Page_261]

48   unsuitable for most species from the upper reaches of rivers, especially the lithophilous ones. [SOURCE: Page_261]

49   Nevertheless, these habitats might be near-optimal or acceptable for a broad series of species [SOURCE: Page_261]

50   that are typical of lowland rivers. This view is supported by several findings that such artificial [SOURCE: Page_261]

51   habitats host a high density of fish (e.g. Townsend & Peirson, 1988; Arlinghaus et al., 2002; [SOURCE: Page_261]

52   Borcherding et al., 2002). [SOURCE: Page_261]

53 [SOURCE: Page_261]

54 [SOURCE: Page_261]

55   The pikeperch (Sander lucioperca L.) is a typical predator of European lowland rivers and [SOURCE: Page_261]

56   lakes, but also of artificial habitats (Deelder & Willemsen, 1964; Linfield & Rickards, 1979). [SOURCE: Page_261]

57   This large percid (max size = c. 130 cm) is important in the functioning of these ecosystems [SOURCE: Page_261]

58   and contributes to inland commercial and recreational fisheries. Its recent extension throughout [SOURCE: Page_261]

59   European watersheds (Craig, 2000) has been attributed to its ability to colonise artificial [SOURCE: Page_261]

3 [SOURCE: Page_261]

60   watersheds, which enable the subsequent colonisation of other drainages. Since pikeperch is a [SOURCE: Page_262]

61   top predator, the impact of its spreading beyond the borders of its natural distribution area [SOURCE: Page_262]

62   might be greater than for other, non-predatory species. A better knowledge of how pikeperch [SOURCE: Page_262]

63   adapts to these artificial, homogenous watersheds is essential to the understanding of its [SOURCE: Page_262]

64   colonising abilities. There is limited knowledge on the habitat preferenda of pikeperch. Only [SOURCE: Page_262]

65   the influences of the depth and the water velocity on habitat use in a reservoir have been [SOURCE: Page_262]

66   described (Vehanen & Lahti, 2003). Other studies on pikeperch behaviour deal with migration [SOURCE: Page_262]

67   and movement (Lehtonen & Toivonen, 1988; Jepsen et al., 1999; Koed et al., 2000; Koed et [SOURCE: Page_262]

68   al., 2002). Habitat use in artificial habitats, and especially artificial canals, is almost unknown. [SOURCE: Page_262]

69 [SOURCE: Page_262]

70 [SOURCE: Page_262]

71   This study investigated the movements and habitat utilisation patterns in spring of pikeperch [SOURCE: Page_262]

72   tagged with acoustic transmitters in a canal network in the Camargue region (River Rhone [SOURCE: Page_262]

73   delta, Southern France), in the south of the species distribution. [SOURCE: Page_262]

74 [SOURCE: Page_262]

75 [SOURCE: Page_262]

76 [SOURCE: Page_262]

77 [SOURCE: Page_262]

78 [SOURCE: Page_262]

79 [SOURCE: Page_262]

80 [SOURCE: Page_262]

81 [SOURCE: Page_262]

82 [SOURCE: Page_262]

83 [SOURCE: Page_262]

4 [SOURCE: Page_262]

## 84                             MATERIAL AND METHODS

## 87    STUDY SITE

88 [SOURCE: Page_263]

89 [SOURCE: Page_263]

90    The study site was located in the Rhône delta, southern France (43°34 N, 4°34 E) (Fig. 1). The [SOURCE: Page_263]

91    Fumemorte canal collects water from a complex canal network spreading over 68 km² [SOURCE: Page_263]

92    (Chauvelon, 1998). The main channel is 14.6 km long and a total of 32.5 km is reached when [SOURCE: Page_263]

93    the largest tributaries are added (Fig. 1). The canal is shallow (1 m), around 14 m wide and has [SOURCE: Page_263]

94   a rectangular section. At its outlet, the Fumemorte canal is equipped with a raisable barrier [SOURCE: Page_263]

95   designed to limit entrance of salt water; the water flows to the Vaccarès lagoon and most [SOURCE: Page_263]

96   aquatic organisms can freely pass from the canal to the lagoon and vice versa as the water [SOURCE: Page_263]

97   flows over the barrier (Rosecchi & Crivelli, 1995). The Vaccarès lagoon is a permanently [SOURCE: Page_263]

98    brackish habitat (salinity > 10 g L-1), while the salinity in the canal varies from 0.1 g L-1 in [SOURCE: Page_263]

99   summer to 5 g L-1 in winter depending on agricultural activities. The water remains highly [SOURCE: Page_263]

100   turbid throughout the year (Secchi depth < 40 cm). Water temperature ranges from 6.5°C to [SOURCE: Page_263]

101   23.5°C (mean monthly values; daily minima and maxima of 1.0 and 27°C, respectively). Banks [SOURCE: Page_263]

102   are colonised by trees, bushes, reeds and grass, but some parts are bare. Except for some [SOURCE: Page_263]

103   narrow ditches, most canals are devoid of aquatic vegetation. [SOURCE: Page_263]

104 [SOURCE: Page_263]

105 [SOURCE: Page_263]

106 [SOURCE: Page_263]

5 [SOURCE: Page_263]

## 107   CAPTURE AND TAGGING

108 [SOURCE: Page_264]

109 [SOURCE: Page_264]

110   Thirteen pikeperch were captured in spring, most of them before the spawning period (i.e. [SOURCE: Page_264]

111   March-April according to Poulet et al. (2004) in the Fumemorte canal (Table I) with fyke nets [SOURCE: Page_264]

112   (mesh size 6 mm) and gill nets (mesh size 40, 55 and 80 mm). [SOURCE: Page_264]

113 [SOURCE: Page_264]

114 [SOURCE: Page_264]

115   Because of the high water conductivity (>1000 µS.cm-1) of the canal, acoustic rather than radio [SOURCE: Page_264]

116   transmitters were used. The transmitters (Lotek CAFT® 11_3) were cylindrical, 40 mm in [SOURCE: Page_264]

117   length, 11 mm in diameter, weighing 9.2 g in air and 4.5 g in water. Transmitters were coded [SOURCE: Page_264]

118   units, tuned on 76.8 KHz and identified with individual codes that enabled the identification of [SOURCE: Page_264]

119   each tagged fish. The pulse rate was set to once every 5 seconds, giving a theoretical [SOURCE: Page_264]

120   operational life of 172 days. [SOURCE: Page_264]

121 [SOURCE: Page_264]

122 [SOURCE: Page_264]

123   Fish were anaesthetised in 0.5 mL.L-1 of 2-phenoxyethanol, measured (LF, nearest millimetre) [SOURCE: Page_264]

124   and weighed (nearest gram). Internal implantation of transmitters was preferred to external [SOURCE: Page_264]

125   attachment after (Koed & Thorstad, 2001) showed that this method did not influence the [SOURCE: Page_264]

126   swimming speed of pikeperch and the other telemetry studies in which no adverse effect was [SOURCE: Page_264]

127   detected (Jepsen et al., 1999; Koed et al., 2000; Koed & Thorstad, 2001). The transmitters [SOURCE: Page_264]

128   were disinfected and surgically implanted into the body cavity through a 20-mm ventral [SOURCE: Page_264]

129   incision posterior to the pelvic girdle. Antibiotic (Duphapen LA®, 0.1 mL.kg-1) was injected [SOURCE: Page_264]

130   into the body cavity. The incision was closed with three absorbable sutures, using Ethicon [SOURCE: Page_264]

6 [SOURCE: Page_264]

131   Vicryl® 2/0. Surgery took about 5 min and all fish recovered their balance and spontaneous [SOURCE: Page_265]

132   swimming within 5 to 10 minutes. They were held overnight in a large tank so as to allow a [SOURCE: Page_265]

133   more complete recovery, then released at the exact places where they were captured. [SOURCE: Page_265]

134 [SOURCE: Page_265]

135 [SOURCE: Page_265]


---
[CHUNK_METADATA]
- Parent_Topic: 47929
- Summary: Technical section focusing on 136   tracking
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High
---
## 136   TRACKING

137 [SOURCE: Page_265]

138 [SOURCE: Page_265]

139   Fish were tracked from a boat using a directional hydrophone connected to a receiver (Lotek [SOURCE: Page_265]

140   model SRX_400). When a fish was detected (at distance of up to 170 m), the engine was [SOURCE: Page_265]

141   turned off and the fish was approached as quietly as possible until the boat stood above the [SOURCE: Page_265]

142   signal’s source. The position of the fish was then recorded as latitude/longitude coordinates [SOURCE: Page_265]

143   with a Global Positioning System (Garmin® GPS 72) receiver installed on the boat’s bow. [SOURCE: Page_265]

144   Preliminary tests using hidden transmitters in the canal showed that mean (± SD) localisation [SOURCE: Page_265]

145   error was 28 cm ± 28 cm when boat was near of the signal source. In practice, such a high [SOURCE: Page_265]

146   accuracy cannot be obtained with live fish, but this suggests that very accurate estimates can be [SOURCE: Page_265]

147   obtained for an undisturbed fish. [SOURCE: Page_265]

148 [SOURCE: Page_265]

149 [SOURCE: Page_265]

150   From late February to early July fish were tracked twice a week (on Tuesdays and Fridays). [SOURCE: Page_265]

151   Tracking sessions started at c.0900 hours and lasted until all fish were detected; this usually [SOURCE: Page_265]

152   took the morning but sometimes longer, until 1700 hours especially in June and July. Fish [SOURCE: Page_265]

153   positions were separated by three or four days. Since pikeperch can travel over more than 1 km [SOURCE: Page_265]

7 [SOURCE: Page_265]

154   in 24 hours (Poulet et al., In press), autocorrelation can be considered as being avoided, i.e. the [SOURCE: Page_266]

155   habitat used at location n+1 is deemed independent from that corresponding to location n. [SOURCE: Page_266]

156 [SOURCE: Page_266]

157 [SOURCE: Page_266]


---

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation

## 158   HABITAT DESCRIPTION

159 [SOURCE: Page_266]

160 [SOURCE: Page_266]

161   The habitat description was performed at the end of the study (July 2002). Four features [SOURCE: Page_266]

162   displaying spatial heterogeneity and likely influencing the fish position were selected: water [SOURCE: Page_266]

163   depth, type of riparian vegetation, height of the vegetation and presence/absence of woody [SOURCE: Page_266]

164   debris. These habitat features were coded into categorical variables (Table II). The survey was [SOURCE: Page_266]

165   done from a boat, using a GPS system for measuring the latitudinal and longitudinal [SOURCE: Page_266]

166   coordinates of points of depth measurements and/or places where vegetation (type and/or [SOURCE: Page_266]

167   height) varied on each bank. The length of the bank was measured for each categorical [SOURCE: Page_266]

168   variable. [SOURCE: Page_266]

169 [SOURCE: Page_266]

170 [SOURCE: Page_266]

171   Fish positions were later plotted on the digitised maps produced from these data. Habitat [SOURCE: Page_266]

172   availability was calculated for each fish by reference to its home range, which is expressed as [SOURCE: Page_266]

173   the distance between the upstreammost and downstreammost locations over the tracking study. [SOURCE: Page_266]

174 [SOURCE: Page_266]

175 [SOURCE: Page_266]

8 [SOURCE: Page_266]

176   The habitat at each fish localisation was noted. Depth and the presence of woody debris were [SOURCE: Page_267]

177   recorded and the bank vegetation was characterised noting the height and percentage of cover [SOURCE: Page_267]

178   of the different types of vegetation. [SOURCE: Page_267]

179 [SOURCE: Page_267]

180 [SOURCE: Page_267]

181   The availability of a habitat type was estimated as the total length for the habitat type within [SOURCE: Page_267]

182   the home range (i.e. the distance between the most upstream and the most downstream [SOURCE: Page_267]

183   localisation) divided by the total length of the home range. The use of a habitat type was [SOURCE: Page_267]

184   estimated as the number of localisation in a habitat type divided by the total number of [SOURCE: Page_267]

185   localisations. [SOURCE: Page_267]

186 [SOURCE: Page_267]

187 [SOURCE: Page_267]

## 188   ANALYSIS

189 [SOURCE: Page_267]

190 [SOURCE: Page_267]

- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation

## 191   HABITAT USED

192 [SOURCE: Page_267]

193 [SOURCE: Page_267]

194   The Friedman test (Friedman, 1937; Iman & Davenport, 1980) was used to assess the habitat [SOURCE: Page_267]

195   preferences of pikeperch in the Fumemorte canal (Alldredge & Ratti, 1986). For each fish and [SOURCE: Page_267]

196   each habitat features (i.e. depth, vegetation type…), the difference between the utilisation of [SOURCE: Page_267]

197   each habitat categorical variables and its availability over the fish’s home range was calculated. [SOURCE: Page_267]

198   Thereafter, these differences were ranked and compared with Friedman’s test. In a first step, [SOURCE: Page_267]

199   the test examines whether the differences between habitat use and availability are [SOURCE: Page_267]

9 [SOURCE: Page_267]

200   homogeneous for all habitats categorical variables (hypothesis H$_1$). To test for this, a T$_2$-value [SOURCE: Page_268]

201   is computed and compared with an F value with (k-1) and (b-1)(k-1) df, where k is the number [SOURCE: Page_268]

202   of animals and b, the number of habitats categorical variables. If H$_1$ is rejected, then pairwise [SOURCE: Page_268]

203   comparisons are performed, using a Fisher’s Least Significant Difference (LSD) procedure on [SOURCE: Page_268]

204   ranks (Alldredge & Ratti, 1986). [SOURCE: Page_268]

205 [SOURCE: Page_268]

206 [SOURCE: Page_268]


---
[CHUNK_METADATA]
- Parent_Topic: 47929
- Summary: Technical section focusing on 207   temporal changes in habitat use
- Key_Entities: Agriculture, Irrigation
- Confidence_Score: High
---
## 207   TEMPORAL CHANGES IN HABITAT USE

208 [SOURCE: Page_268]

209 [SOURCE: Page_268]

210   Habitat selection by fish being a multivariate process, a Principal Component Analysis on [SOURCE: Page_268]

211   Instrumental Variables (PCAIV) (Rao, 1964; Sabatier et al., 1989) was used to i) test for the [SOURCE: Page_268]

212   variability of habitat use according to the period (month) taking into account the individual [SOURCE: Page_268]

213   dimension and ii) test for the variability of habitat use according to the individual taking into [SOURCE: Page_268]

214   account the period. PCAIV is a combination of conventional multivariate ordination and [SOURCE: Page_268]

215   multiple regression analysis. Significance was tested by a permutation test: 1000 random [SOURCE: Page_268]

216   permutations between the rows (i.e. a row being an individual i at a day j) from all groups [SOURCE: Page_268]

217   (depending on the factor tested) were performed and the PCAIV variance was calculated for [SOURCE: Page_268]

218   each case. The p-value is given by the frequency of simulated variance greater than or equal to [SOURCE: Page_268]

219   the observed variance. Multivariate analyses were performed using ADE-4 software [SOURCE: Page_268]

220   (Thioulouse et al., 1997). [SOURCE: Page_268]

10 [SOURCE: Page_268]

## 224   SURVIVAL OF TAGGED FISH

225 [SOURCE: Page_269]

226 [SOURCE: Page_269]

227   Contact with three individuals (N°46, 156 and 162) was lost shortly before the beginning of the [SOURCE: Page_269]

228   tracking programme. Transmitter N°46 was subsequently found in the water under a great [SOURCE: Page_269]

229   cormorant (Phalacrocorax carbo) resting place sited on the bank of the Fumemorte canal. [SOURCE: Page_269]

230   Pikeperch N°170 was captured by an angler in the Fumemorte canal in June and N°86 was [SOURCE: Page_269]

231   caught by a fisherman in the Vaccarès lagoon in October. As a result, the movements of 10 fish [SOURCE: Page_269]

232   were analysed (Table I). [SOURCE: Page_269]

233 [SOURCE: Page_269]

234 [SOURCE: Page_269]

## 235   MOVEMENTS AND POSITIONS IN THE FUMEMORTE NETWORK

236 [SOURCE: Page_269]

237 [SOURCE: Page_269]

238   From March to early July, no pikeperch was detected in the Vaccarès lagoon. The distribution [SOURCE: Page_269]

239   of pikeperch within the Fumemorte network was not homogeneous (Fig. 2). Some places were [SOURCE: Page_269]

240   occupied by a single tagged fish whereas several others were used by many tracked fish of both [SOURCE: Page_269]

241   sexes. In June-early July, many pikeperch were localised near the water inlet. Over the entire [SOURCE: Page_269]

242   tracking session, the home ranges of females and males overlapped, but males occurred more [SOURCE: Page_269]

243   frequently in the upper part of the Fumemorte canal, whereas females were more frequently [SOURCE: Page_269]

244   located in its lower section and in tributaries (Fig. 3). Overlaps between home ranges varied [SOURCE: Page_269]

11 [SOURCE: Page_269]

245   substantially during the season, being maximal during the spawning season and decreasing [SOURCE: Page_270]

246   progressively thereafter, as females ranged further downstream during the flow increase in [SOURCE: Page_270]

247   spring while males remained within or close to their early spring range. This general trend did [SOURCE: Page_270]

248   not apply to all females, since for example, female N°73 showed a consistent fidelity to a [SOURCE: Page_270]

249   specific place and homed to this place, in contrast to female N°96, which exhibited the most [SOURCE: Page_270]

250   frequent and longest movements. [SOURCE: Page_270]

251 [SOURCE: Page_270]

252 [SOURCE: Page_270]

## 253   DRAINAGE NETWORK OCCUPATION AND HABITAT PREFERENCES

254 [SOURCE: Page_270]

255 [SOURCE: Page_270]

256   Pikeperch avoided depths less than 1 m and showed a preference for depth between 1 and 1.5 [SOURCE: Page_270]

257   m (T22,18=33.9, p<0.001; Table III, Fig. 4). Significant differences were also found for the type [SOURCE: Page_270]

258   (T23,27=5.6, p<0.05) and height of riparian vegetation (T22,18=6.7, p<0.05). Pikeperch [SOURCE: Page_270]

259   significantly preferred places with trees and vegetation higher than 3 m and avoided areas [SOURCE: Page_270]

260   where vegetation height was between 0.5 and 1.49 m (Table III, Fig. 4). On average, forty nine [SOURCE: Page_270]

261   percent (± 7 S.E.) of the localisations of the tracked fish were in or close to woody debris. [SOURCE: Page_270]

262 [SOURCE: Page_270]

263 [SOURCE: Page_270]

## 264   HABITAT COMPARISON BETWEEN SPRING PERIODS AND TAGGED FISH

265 [SOURCE: Page_270]

266 [SOURCE: Page_270]

267   Habitat utilisation patterns varied slightly but significantly between the months of the study [SOURCE: Page_270]

268   (variance = 5.3 %; p<0.001; Fig. 5). In March, pikeperch were located in the parts of the canal [SOURCE: Page_270]

12 [SOURCE: Page_270]

269   where the bank vegetation was dominated by bushes. In April and May, pikeperch occurred in [SOURCE: Page_271]

270   shallower parts with trees (or high vegetation) or without vegetation but with woody debris. [SOURCE: Page_271]

271   During June and early July, they were frequently found in deeper parts in tributaries with bank [SOURCE: Page_271]

272   vegetation of grass and reed. The PCAIV revealed significant between-individual differences [SOURCE: Page_271]

273   (variance = 8.4 %; p<0.001; Fig. 6). Some fish were localised near trees and/or high vegetation [SOURCE: Page_271]

274   and woody debris mainly whereas others were found near banks with grass and reeds. Some [SOURCE: Page_271]

275   individuals were distributed along the second axis which contrasts depth with bushes. There [SOURCE: Page_271]

276   were no obvious between-sex differences (Fig. 6). [SOURCE: Page_271]

277 [SOURCE: Page_271]

278 [SOURCE: Page_271]
