---
## 279                                         DISCUSSION

280 [SOURCE: Page_271]

281 [SOURCE: Page_271]

282   Out of thirteen tagged fish, ten were successfully tracked. One pikeperch was probably taken [SOURCE: Page_271]

283   by a great cormorant, which has already been reported to consume adult pikeperch (Santoul et [SOURCE: Page_271]

284   al., 2004). During and after the study, two pikeperch were caught by anglers and another one [SOURCE: Page_271]

285   by a professional fisherman. The fate of the other tagged fish is unknown, but there is no [SOURCE: Page_271]

286   evidence that the tagging protocol adversely affected the fish. [SOURCE: Page_271]

287 [SOURCE: Page_271]

288 [SOURCE: Page_271]

289   On several occasions, many tagged pikeperch were localised within the same stretch of the [SOURCE: Page_271]

290   canal (c. < 50m), thereby suggested some form of aggregative behaviour. This hypothesis was [SOURCE: Page_271]

291   supported empirically by the capture of 11 pikeperch ranging from 10 to 74 cm LF during the [SOURCE: Page_271]

292   recapture of pikeperch N°74, within a stretch of no more than 30 m in a small tributary. This [SOURCE: Page_271]

13 [SOURCE: Page_271]

293   behaviour is consistent with earlier studies which described pikeperch clumped at particular [SOURCE: Page_272]

294   places (Deelder & Willemsen, 1964; Jepsen et al., 1999). Clumping of captures or acoustic [SOURCE: Page_272]

295   locations is not synonymous with schooling behaviour. It might merely reflect the [SOURCE: Page_272]

296   correspondence between the habitat preferenda of fish and the scarcity of preferred habitats. [SOURCE: Page_272]

297   The observation of aggregation suggests that even though only 10 fish were tracked during this [SOURCE: Page_272]

298   study, the information on behaviour and habitat preferences collected during this study is [SOURCE: Page_272]

299   probably valid for many other pikeperch in the Fumemorte network. [SOURCE: Page_272]

300 [SOURCE: Page_272]

301 [SOURCE: Page_272]

302   No pikeperch was detected in the Vaccarès lagoon during spring and early summer. The [SOURCE: Page_272]

303   Vaccarès lagoon provides a suitable substratum (sand, macrophytes) for spawning but its [SOURCE: Page_272]

304   salinity (>10 g.l-1) is probably too high for the reproduction of pikeperch (Lappalainen et al., [SOURCE: Page_272]

305   2003). This does not preclude the possibility that pikeperch colonise the Vaccarès lagoon at [SOURCE: Page_272]

306   other times of the year which is seemingly the case since a tagged fish was recaptured by a [SOURCE: Page_272]

307   fisherman in the lagoon in October after a high flow period. [SOURCE: Page_272]

308 [SOURCE: Page_272]

309 [SOURCE: Page_272]

310   Despite an apparent homogeneity of habitats in the canal, pikeperch displayed significant [SOURCE: Page_272]

311   habitat selection preferences in term of depth and riparian vegetation. However, in the [SOURCE: Page_272]

312   Fumemorte network, places deeper than 1 m and sited under trees or high vegetation amount to [SOURCE: Page_272]

313   no more than 10% of the network’s surface investigated. This suggests that the carrying [SOURCE: Page_272]

314   capacity for adult pikeperch is low in the Fumemorte network. The habitat preferenda [SOURCE: Page_272]

315   highlighted here are in accordance with the observation made by Koed et al. (2000). Deep, [SOURCE: Page_272]

316   structured habitats below trees or among roots, log jams and woody debris represent natural [SOURCE: Page_272]

14 [SOURCE: Page_272]

317   shelter against predation (Wesche et al., 1987; Inoue & Nakano, 1998; Crook & Robertson, [SOURCE: Page_273]

318   1999; Allouche, 2002) especially avian predation (Crook & Robertson, 1999; Allouche & [SOURCE: Page_273]

319   Gaudin, 2001). Fish predators of adult pikeperch are not numerous, however, the Camargue is [SOURCE: Page_273]

320   an important wintering and nesting area for many piscivorous birds such as the great [SOURCE: Page_273]

321   cormorant. Note that before tagging, six of the tracked pikeperch displayed scars that were [SOURCE: Page_273]

322   assumed to have been caused by cormorants. [SOURCE: Page_273]

323 [SOURCE: Page_273]

324 [SOURCE: Page_273]

325   In March, at the peak of the spawning period (Poulet et al., 2004), males and females were [SOURCE: Page_273]

326   both upstream in the main canal, near banks with bushes indicating a putative spawning area. [SOURCE: Page_273]

327   Spawning takes place in a nest that is previously prepared by the male. The eggs are sensitive [SOURCE: Page_273]

328   to low oxygen concentrations and require a clean spawning substratum even though the male [SOURCE: Page_273]

329   also takes care of the eggs and young fry (Deelder & Willemsen, 1964; Sonesten, 1991; Craig, [SOURCE: Page_273]

330   2000). In the Fumemorte canal, the bottom is composed of clay and silt, which are unsuitable [SOURCE: Page_273]

331   for pikeperch spawning. The sole substratum that can be used for spawning in the Fumemorte [SOURCE: Page_273]

332   canal are roots and other woody debris (Jepsen et al., 1999; Koed et al., 2000). This may also [SOURCE: Page_273]

333   account for the marked preference of pikeperch for debris, at least during the spawning season. [SOURCE: Page_273]

334 [SOURCE: Page_273]

335   The downstream movements of females and their entry into tributaries, which occurred after [SOURCE: Page_273]

336   spawning, can be viewed as a trophic migration. In the Fumemorte canal the main prey for [SOURCE: Page_273]

337   adult pikeperch is sandsmelt (Atherina boyeri R.) (Poulet et al., 2004). The sandsmelt start [SOURCE: Page_273]

338   colonizing the Fumemorte canal in November, they spawn from April to June both in the [SOURCE: Page_273]

339   tributaries and in the marshes, and from June onwards adults and young-of-the year return to [SOURCE: Page_273]

340   the Vaccarès lagoon (Rosecchi & Crivelli, 1995). It is likely that pikeperch track the [SOURCE: Page_273]

15 [SOURCE: Page_273]

341   migrations of its prey as it was observed by Koed et al. (2000) in a Danish river. Further data, [SOURCE: Page_274]

342   in particular on sandsmelt abundance and movement, are needed to validate this hypothesis. [SOURCE: Page_274]

343 [SOURCE: Page_274]

344 [SOURCE: Page_274]

345   While females moved downstream, males remained upstream in the main canal. Such gender- [SOURCE: Page_274]

346   dependent differences in spatial behaviour has already been observed (Jepsen et al., 1999; [SOURCE: Page_274]

347   Koed et al., 2000 ) and attributed to the parental care that is displayed by males pikeperch [SOURCE: Page_274]

348   (Lappalainen et al., 2003), while females display an increasing feeding activity after spawning [SOURCE: Page_274]

349   (Jepsen et al., 2000). In the Fumemorte network, some females were more active and had a [SOURCE: Page_274]

350   longer home range than others. These observations agree with those of Fickling & Lee (1985) [SOURCE: Page_274]

351   that pikeperch populations comprise two components with contrasting mobility patterns. [SOURCE: Page_274]

352 [SOURCE: Page_274]

353 [SOURCE: Page_274]

354   In June and July, three tagged pikeperch occupied regularly sections with turbulent water [SOURCE: Page_274]

355   movement and two others visited these places punctually (three among the ten having been lost [SOURCE: Page_274]

356   before this date). Fish sampling in these habitats revealed other untagged pikeperch and a low [SOURCE: Page_274]

357   concentration of potential fish prey, thereby suggesting that these places were appreciated but [SOURCE: Page_274]

358   not for foraging purposes. In the Fumemorte canal, oxygen concentration drops regularly under [SOURCE: Page_274]

359   3 mg.L-1 during summer (Crivelli, Unpub. data) which is much lower than the critical threshold [SOURCE: Page_274]

360   for pikeperch (around 7 mg.L-1 at 20°C, according to Dolinin (1974)). Consequently, these [SOURCE: Page_274]

361   turbulent places might be important refuges during periods of critically low oxygen [SOURCE: Page_274]

362   concentration as it was observed by Jepsen et al. (1999) in a Danish reservoir. [SOURCE: Page_274]

363 [SOURCE: Page_274]

16 [SOURCE: Page_274]

364   This study demonstrated that pikeperch in an artificial, seemingly homogeneous environment, [SOURCE: Page_275]

365   display habitat preferenda and select deep or structured habitats, either for spawning or refuge [SOURCE: Page_275]

366   purposes. Provided that such habitats be present, which is likely in artificial channels, [SOURCE: Page_275]

367   pikeperch might colonise these channels and probably establish viable populations, which [SOURCE: Page_275]

368   promotes their spread through water drainages, even though the connecting network is long [SOURCE: Page_275]

369   and largely exceeds their annual home range. As a corollary, resident pikeperch populations in [SOURCE: Page_275]

370   artificial channel networks will be strongly affected by the management of riparian vegetation [SOURCE: Page_275]

371   and debris. Pikeperch display a plastic behaviour which allows it to adapt to difficult [SOURCE: Page_275]

372   hydrosystems. This study provides some guidelines to increase habitat suitability within [SOURCE: Page_275]

373   artificial hydrosystems, particularly by favouring the development of the riparian vegetation [SOURCE: Page_275]

374   and woody debris. [SOURCE: Page_275]

375 [SOURCE: Page_275]

376 [SOURCE: Page_275]

## ANNEXE 4

Annexe 4 Poulet N, Forgeois S, Crivelli AJ, Lek S, and Argillier C. 2004. Life history traits of the pikeperch (Sander lucioperca L.) in the southern outskirt of its distribution area. In Barry TP and Malison JA, eds. Proceedings of Percis III: The Third International Percid Fish Symposium. Madison, Wi: University of Wisconsin Sea Grant Institute. [SOURCE: Page_291]

285 [SOURCE: Page_291]

286 [SOURCE: Page_292]

LIFE HISTORY TRAITS OF THE PIKEPERCH Sander lucioperca (L.) IN THE SOUTHERN OUTSKIRT OF ITS DISTRIBUTION AREA [SOURCE: Page_293]

Nicolas Poulet^1, Stéphane Forgeois^1, Alain J. Crivelli^2, Sovan Lek^3 & Christine Argillier^1 [SOURCE: Page_293]

1 Cemagref, 361, rue JF Breton BP 5095 34033 Montpellier Cedex France. [EMAIL_REDACTED] [SOURCE: Page_293]

2 La Tour du Valat, 13200 Arles, France. [SOURCE: Page_293]

3 LaDyBio, 118 route de Narbonne 31062 Toulouse cedex 4, France. [SOURCE: Page_293]

Introduction. In 1948 the pikeperch colonised the sandy Vaccarès lagoon, in the Mediterranean delta of the Rhône River (Goubier 1972). Very few data are available about the life history traits of pikeperch in the southern part of its distribution area. Yet, such information is crucial for the species management. We investigated growth, maturity, fecundity and the diet of one of the Rhône delta population. [SOURCE: Page_293]

Methods. The study area was located in the Rhône delta (Fig. 1), southern France (43°34 N, 4°34 E). The Fumemorte canal collects water from a complex canal network spreading over 68 km². It was shallow (1 m), around 14 m wide and flowed into the brackish Vaccarès lagoon. The salinity in the canal varied from 0.1 gl-1 in summer to 5 gl-1 in winter depending mainly on the agricultural activities. The water remained highly turbid all over the year. [SOURCE: Page_293]

N FRANCE 0      5      10 Km G ra nd R hô [SOURCE: Page_294]

ne ne hô R tit [SOURCE: Page_294]

Vaccarès Pe [SOURCE: Page_294]

Lagoon [SOURCE: Page_294]

Mediterranean Sea [SOURCE: Page_294]

Figure 1. The Rhône delta (the Camargue). The rectangle delimite the Fumemorte basin. [SOURCE: Page_294]

Age readings were performed on scales and otoliths of 30 males and 46 females captured from 2000 to 2002. The growth curve was fitted using the Schnute (1981) model which includes numerous historical models as special cases and the fitting was processed by non-linear regression based on the least squared errors. Sexual cycle was determined by monitoring the Gonadosomatic Index (i.e. weight of ovaries*100/total weight). Length at first maturity was considered as the length at which 50% of the individuals were mature using a logistic regression. Both sexual cycle and maturity were studied using pikeperch collected since 1982 (more than 300 fish). The fecundity was measured on 17 females captured between January and March 2000 and was estimated by weighting 500 eggs to the nearest 1/10 mg in a tarred water volume. As the weight of the eggs changed among month, its relation with the fork length was tested using a covariance analyse where the length was the covariable and the month the fixed factor. Diet [SOURCE: Page_294]

analysis was performed on 194 pikeperch caught between 1982 and 2002. The occurrence of each prey was registered. [SOURCE: Page_295]

Results. No growth difference between sexes was observed (ANCOVA, F$_1$=1.28, p=0.26). Richards growth model was the best to fit pikeperch growth in the Fumemorte canal, both sexes pooled (Fig. 2; n=76, r²=0.94; p<0.001). The estimated fork length at 1 year was 258mm, 2: 404mm, 3: 568mm, 4: 689mm and 5 years: 747mm. The estimated Linf was 779mm. [SOURCE: Page_295]

The spawning occurred from mid-March to late April, at a temperature of 12°C-15°C (Fig. 3). A sexual rest occurred in summer and the ovaries maturation took place from September to March. [SOURCE: Page_295]

Fifty percent of the males reached their sexual maturity at 246mm (11 months) and 50% of the female at 322mm (18 months) (Fig. 4). The smallest mature male and female were 215mm and 242mm long respectively. [SOURCE: Page_296]

The mean relative fecundity was 255412 (± 16710) eggs/kg. The absolute fecundity increased significantly with the fork length according to a power function (n=17, r²=0.77, p<0.001), while the relative fecundity decreased significantly according to a linear function (n=17, r²=0.46, p=0.002) (Fig. 5). The egg weight (n=17, mean=0.53mg ± 0.06) increased significantly with the fork length (ANCOVA, F1=9.8, p=0.008). [SOURCE: Page_297]

On 194 stomachs investigated, 107 were full and 89.7%, 11.2% and 2.8% contained fish, crustacean and other preys, respectively. Among the 64 stomachs displaying identifiable species 58% contained Atherina boyeri, 19% shrimp and 14% Mugil spp. Other species as Lepomis gibbosus, Carassius gibelio, Alburnus alburnus, Abramis spp. and Pseudorasbora parva were also found. No cannibalism was observed. [SOURCE: Page_298]

Discussion. The growth of the pikeperch in the Fumemorte canal was one of the fastest observed for this species. This might be due to the geographical location: summer water temperatures at this latitude are close to the pikeperch physiological optimum (i.e. 27°C, Hokanson 1977). Thus 0+ become piscivorous early which speeds up their growth. The age at first maturity was also among the earliest reported but the usual difference between males and females was still observed. As for growth, the acquisition of the sexual maturity probably depended on the latitude: earlier maturity is related to fastest growth rate at lower latitudes (Lappalainen et al. 2003). The power function fitting the absolute fecundity and length was congruent with an early sexual maturity (Zivkov & Petrova 1993). Smaller females had a greater relative fecundity which [SOURCE: Page_298]

was seldom observed for this species (Lappalainen et al. 2003). This may be due to the short lifespan of the pikeperch in the Fumemorte canal bringing the females to express their full fecundity potential at their youngest age. However, older females displayed heaviest eggs: this is commonly observed for teleost fish. Further data on fecundity and egg size would be necessary to confirm these results. In the Fumemorte canal, adult pikeperch fed on fish but also on shrimp. Sandsmelt and mullet were only abundant in winter. Nevertheless, other species abundant all over the year also provide suitable preys. This fact associated with the low density of pikeperch could explain why no cannibalism occurred. Environmental conditions in the Rhône delta bring the pikeperch to adapt its life history traits thereby proving its plasticity. [SOURCE: Page_299]

Acknowledgements. Great thanks to P. Contournet for the sampling and to J. Lappalainen and P. Irz for their valuable comments. [SOURCE: Page_299]

Le sandre (Sander lucioperca (L.)) : Biologie, comportement et dynamique des populations en Camargue (Bouches du Rhône, France) [SOURCE: Page_300]

Résumé [SOURCE: Page_300]

Le sandre est un Percidé largement répandu en Europe. Le fonctionnement de ses populations méridionales est cependant mal connu. L'objectif de ce travail est d'apporter des connaissances nouvelles sur l'écologie du sandre par l'étude de la population d’un réseau de canaux en Camargue (sud de la France). Différentes techniques ont été mises en œuvre pour étudier l’isolement de la population (génétique, morphométrie), l’utilisation de l’habitat (télémétrie), la biologie (traits de vie) et la dynamique dans le système. Le fonctionnement de cette population dépend plus de facteurs biotiques qu’abiotiques (contrairement à ce qui est observé sur les populations septentrionales), ainsi que de facteurs anthropiques (agriculture…). [SOURCE: Page_300]

Mots clés : Sander lucioperca, génétique, morphométrie, télémétrie, migration, habitat, croissance, traits d’histoire de vie, recrutement, dynamique de population, canal, irrigation, drainage. [SOURCE: Page_300]

The pikeperch (Sander lucioperca (L.)): Biology, behaviour and populations dynamics in Camargue (Bouches du Rhône, France) [SOURCE: Page_300]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_300]

Pikeperch is a Percid fish widely distributed in Europe, but little is known about the ecology of its most southern populations. The aim of this study was to provide new ecological knowledge on pikeperch by studying a population dwelling a canal network in Camargue (Southern France). A variety of methods were used to assess the isolation of the population (genetics, morphometrics), habitat use (telemetry), biology (life history traits) and dynamics in the system. The population’s functioning is more dependent upon biotic than abiotic factors, which contrasts with the observations made on the Nothern populations, and displays anthropogenic impacts (agriculture…). [SOURCE: Page_300]

Key words: Sander lucioperca, genetic, morphometric, telemetry, migration, habitat, growth, life history traits, recruitment, population dynamics, canal, irrigation, drainage. [SOURCE: Page_300]
